Title: Detecting Generated-Depth Injection Attacks on RGB-D Place Authentication

  • Authors: Seigo Kawachi, Satoshi Matsuura
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01550
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01550
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01550]] Detecting Generated-Depth Injection Attacks on RGB-D Place Authentication(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01550)
  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: Advances in monocular depth estimation (MDE) now let an attacker generate depth from publicly available RGB images and submit forged RGB-D queries. Such attacks break a core assumption of RGB-D place authentication: that depth comes from a sensor. We study a presentation attack detection (PAD) classifier that uses geometric consistency between the enrollment and query views, as a software-layer defense. A three-stage evaluation shows how far this classifier detects software-injected depth, depending on how accurately the attacker knows the camera intrinsics. In the first stage, the attacker has only an estimate of the intrinsics, and the PAD classifier separates forgeries generated by all three MDE models with a validation area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.997-0.999. In the second stage, the attacker obtains the exact intrinsics, and the original PAD classifier falls to near chance (AUC 0.54-0.61); a retrained PAD classifier, however, still has the registration residual between enrollment and query as a cue. In the third stage, an oracle evaluation, in which the evaluator fits warps using the ground-truth 3D geometry, shows that detection falls to near chance only in its strongest case, where every valid query keypoint is given its ground-truth 3D coordinate. Of the detection cues, the reprojection error captures the attacker's misestimate of the intrinsics, whereas the registration residual remains even when the attacker knows the exact intrinsics.

Title: Generative Modeling with Continuous Flows: Sample Complexity of Flow Matching

  • Authors: Mudit Gaur, Prashant Trivedi, Shuchin Aeron, Amrit Singh Bedi, George K. Atia, Vaneet Aggarwal
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01559
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01559
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01559]] Generative Modeling with Continuous Flows: Sample Complexity of Flow Matching(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01559)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Flow matching has emerged as a promising alternative to diffusion-based generative modeling, offering a continuous-time ordinary differential equation formulation with fast sampling and a simple regression-based training objective. Despite its empirical success, some theoretical analyses idealize the learning procedure by assuming that the velocity field is obtained by exact empirical risk minimization. This assumption does not reflect the practical finite-dataset setting, where the empirical flow-matching loss is only approximately minimized by a finite number of stochastic-gradient updates. We develop an empirical-risk-minimizer-free analysis for flow matching that explicitly accounts for both finite-sample statistical error and finite-time empirical optimization error. Our analysis considers a finite dataset of \(N\) flow-matching samples and a learned parameter \(\theta_T\) obtained after \(T\) stochastic gradient updates on the empirical objective. Under regularity assumptions on the interpolation path, sub-exponential source and target distributions, approximation by a bounded-parameter smooth neural velocity class, empirical-loss optimization, and uniform flow stability, we prove a guarantee that holds with high probability over the finite training dataset and in expectation over the stochastic-gradient randomness. The resulting bound separates approximation error, statistical error from the finite dataset, tail error from unbounded source and target distributions, and empirical optimization error from approximate minimization of the finite-sample objective.

Title: Finite-Time Online Sample Complexity for Conditional Diffusion Models

  • Authors: Mudit Gaur, Prashant Trivedi, Sasidhar Kunapuli, Amrit Singh Bedi, Vaneet Aggarwal
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01562
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01562
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01562]] Finite-Time Online Sample Complexity for Conditional Diffusion Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01562)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: We study the finite-time training and sampling error of conditional diffusion models trained by online stochastic-gradient denoising score matching. Rather than assuming access to an exact empirical or population risk minimizer, we analyze the score network produced after a finite number of stochastic-gradient updates under an explicit localized Polyak--Łojasiewicz (PL) condition, stochastic-gradient regularity, and a trajectory-containment assumption. Our analysis separates the network approximation complexity from the online training-sample budget and propagates both errors through the reverse diffusion process. Under anisotropic smoothness, a joint intrinsic-dimensional structure for the data and conditioning variables, sub-exponential tails, and explicit approximation-class compatibility, we obtain a conditional score approximation error of order \[ \widetilde{\mathcal O}\!\left(M^{-\eta_{\mathrm{app}}}\right), \qquad \eta_{\mathrm{app}} = \frac{2\alpha_x} {2\alpha_x+d_x'+(\alpha_x/\alpha_y)d_y'}, \] where \(M\) denotes the approximation complexity, \(d_x'\) and \(d_y'\) are the intrinsic response and conditioning dimensions, and \(\alpha_x,\alpha_y\) are their corresponding smoothness exponents. For \(N=RB\) online denoising samples used by SGD, the finite-time optimization error is controlled explicitly in terms of the PL, smoothness, gradient-noise, and initialization constants. Combining these ingredients with reverse-process stability yields a total-variation bound that decomposes into terminal-initialization, time-discretization, approximation/localization, and finite-time optimization errors.

Title: Meta-memorization and memorization scaling laws in transformers

  • Authors: Alex Nguyen, Kenneth A. Norman, Gautam Reddy
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01564
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01564
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01564]] Meta-memorization and memorization scaling laws in transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01564)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Larger transformers can memorize more data, with a capacity (in bits) that scales roughly linearly with the number of parameters. Counterintuitively, larger models also memorize faster, requiring fewer repetitions of a sequence during training before it is memorized. We reproduce both memorization scaling laws in transformers trained on random binary sequences, showing that rapid memorization can arise even when data contain no semantic or compositional structure. Rapid memorization is due to a meta-memorization effect, where models trained to memorize a large set of random sequences learn to memorize novel sequences more quickly. The rate $r(t)$ at which models memorize grows with training time $t$ as a power-law $r(t) \sim t^{\beta}$ with a fixed exponent $\beta$ but an increasing upper cutoff across model scales. Meta-memorization enables even one-layer transformers to few-shot memorize a 100-bit sequence. This effect is partly explained by the formation of structured attention heads that convey position-invariant sequence information to MLPs where memorized sequences are stored. Meta-memorization is recapitulated in a simplified model of a dense associative memory where retrieval is constrained by an attentional bottleneck. Our results suggest transformers, through pre-training, acquire reusable mechanisms that enable highly efficient storage and retrieval of memories.

Title: Beyond Validation Loss: Coverage and Reliability Dynamics in Robot Policy Pretraining

  • Authors: Zhanyi Sun, Max Simchowitz, Shuran Song
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01565
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01565
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01565]] Beyond Validation Loss: Coverage and Reliability Dynamics in Robot Policy Pretraining(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01565)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: In modern robot learning, generative robot policies are first pretrained through supervised learning from demonstrations, then refined through reinforcement learning, supervised finetuning, or other forms of posttraining. Yet conventional pretraining metrics, such as validation loss and average task success, provide an incomplete account of which policies support both effective direct deployment and subsequent posttraining. In this work, we study closed-loop pass@k, which evaluates the probability that k rollouts from the same initial condition attain at least one success. Thus, pass@1 measures mean task success; large-k pass@k measures whether success remains reachable under repeated sampling. Across a wide range of posttraining recipes, we show that large-k pass@k reveals posttraining differences not captured by pass@1. In particular, checkpoints with similar pass@1 but different large-k pass@k have different posttraining performance. Moreover, we show pass@k peaks at different checkpoints during supervised pretraining, revealing a coverage-reliability tradeoff over the course of training. Based on these observations, we propose two practical interventions: (1) a purely offline checkpoint-selection criterion that balances coverage and reliability without closed-loop evaluation, and (2) a generative data augmentation procedure that expands supervision beyond the finite demonstration set, preserving large-k pass@k while maintaining or improving pass@1 and raising the observed coverage-reliability Pareto frontier. Together, our results motivate evaluation, checkpoint selection, and training procedures that explicitly account for posttraining. Blog post, visualizations, and code are available at this https URL.

Title: Search-Aware On-Policy Self-Distillation with Sibling Alignment for Generative Recommendation

  • Authors: Rongfeng Guo, Zhuang Zhuang, Ji Dai, Peng Zhao, Shijie Li, Jie Chen, Teng Long, Vincent Tao Hu, Fei Pan
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01568
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01568
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01568]] Search-Aware On-Policy Self-Distillation with Sibling Alignment for Generative Recommendation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01568)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Generative recommendation retrieves items by autoregressively generating semantic IDs, typically via beam search. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) enhances training supervision by transferring privileged self-teacher knowledge at student-generated states. However, beam-search retention depends on competition among prefixes: visited states differ in importance, and initially reliable decisions may approach the pruning boundary as training evolves. Moreover, conventional distribution matching couples target-token confidence with preferences among competing continuations, potentially weakening relational supervision when the self-teacher strongly favors the target. To address these challenges, we propose Search-Aware On-Policy Self-Distillation (SA-OPSD), a framework that transfers privileged self-teacher knowledge to the states visited during the student's own beam search. Specifically, we introduce Evolving-Boundary Sampling, which prioritizes search-critical states by considering both current pruning-boundary proximity and initial-reference retention-margin decline under a controlled distillation budget. Furthermore, we develop Confidence-Decoupled Branch Distillation, which separates target-probability calibration from informative sibling relations to better preserve the teacher's preferences among competing branches. Experiments on Industrial, Office, and Instruments demonstrate strong performance for SA-OPSD.

Title: StateGate: A Behavioral-Fidelity Prompt Optimization Method for Long-Chain LLM Agents

  • Abstract: Currently, long-chain LLM agents commonly suffer from behavioral degradation in complex tasks, where prompts are written in full but steps are not executed in full. To address this issue, existing approaches face three major challenges. First, constraints are strongly bound to their textual carriers; once natural-language constraint sentences are removed, their binding force vanishes. Second, the execution process lacks independent verification, as the evidence for judgment is often supplied by the executing party itself, leading to the failure of self-referential validation. Third, existing remediation schemes are difficult to generalize, and hard-coded mechanisms cannot be transferred across tasks. In this paper, we propose StateGate, a novel prompt-optimization scheme that transforms semantic constraints into symbolic gates enforced by an external deterministic runtime. By decoupling the content of a constraint from its carrier of efficacy, StateGate achieves behavioral fidelity in long-chain tasks without losing the binding force of the prompt. Specifically, we first propose four independent compression strategies (deduplication, generalization, externalization, and text-to-gating) together with their corresponding decision chains, enabling principled prompt symbolization. Second, we design a three-component architecture consisting of a state file, a validator, and an orchestrator, which thoroughly separates judgment authority from execution authority. Third, we formally prove that the compliance of symbolic constraints is independent of context size. Finally, we validate the framework on a vulnerability-discovery task in the security domain; the results show that StateGate achieves 100% execution closure and accurate vulnerability detection.

A handful of very large incidents drives this concentration, yet it still holds when we switch to other loss bases or reclassify the boundary cases. By tying what attackers do to the bridge functions and security assumptions they target, the architecture gives threat modelling for cross-chain bridges an empirical footing.

Title: NatureFusion: Rethinking Infrared and Visible Image Fusion as Natural Complementary Enhancement

Last but not least, to further improve downstream performance without sacrificing natural appearance, we freeze the fusion adapter and introduce a detector-supervised task adapter that strengthens the complementary contribution of infrared. Extensive comparisons with seven multi-modal fusion and seven single-modality enhancement methods demonstrate that NatureFusion generates visually natural fused images with the best perceptual quality. Detection and segmentation models pretrained on visible images achieve substantially higher accuracy on our fused images than on those of any competing method.

  • Abstract: Anticipating financial outcomes requires synthesizing evolving evidence and reasoning under uncertainty, making deep research systems promising tools for forecasting. Title: Yielding Fairness: Coupled Learning and Fairness for Value-Weighted Ranking

  • Authors: Ami Tavory, Yaniv Sheena, Shaked Bar, Yair Zagdansky
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01647
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01647
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01647]] Yielding Fairness: Coupled Learning and Fairness for Value-Weighted Ranking(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01647)
  • Keywords: protect, fair
  • Abstract: Risk screening and recommendation systems pass or block accounts that generate both value and loss. Under a blocked-value budget, a cap on expected value forgone through blocking, the optimal ranking orders accounts by expected value per unit expected loss, a ratio of conditional expectations. This rule can conflict with accepted harm or net-harm criteria, blocking accounts that another criterion would pass at the same budget. We call these cases account-level objections and define group complaint disparity as the difference between groups' normalized realized objections. Weighted binary cross-entropy (W-BCE) implements the value-per-loss order with one score. Objection penalties modify the ranking, while group multipliers scale those penalties differently by group, tracing tradeoffs between loss capture and complaint disparity. This preserves the value-budgeted ranking structure and makes the cost of reducing contestable blocks explicit. Across public recommendation, insurance, credit, and mortgage datasets, we compare undamped W-BCE with ordinary BCE using yield Gini, an area summary of loss capture versus blocked value. Logarithmic weight damping is evaluated separately. Held-out evaluations measure capture-disparity tradeoffs. Validation-selected group-aware complaint penalties improve complaint closure in some cohorts. A corrected comparison normalizes and enlarges candidate policy banks using validation-only price sweeps and retains all ten applicable sources. The source-level results do not establish a statistically significant average advantage over group-blind pricing at the fixed minimum retained loss capture. We also compare with blocked-count, blocked-value, and protected-loss parity postprocessors, which do not directly constrain realized objections, and measure objections in candidate-count and loss units.

Title: Physics-Informed One-Step Generation on Long-short flow maps

  • Authors: Zhiqi Li, Duowen Chen, Jinjin He, Bo Zhu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01651
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01651
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01651]] Physics-Informed One-Step Generation on Long-short flow maps(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01651)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Physics-informed generative models should produce samples that are both data-aligned and physically valid, yet existing methods are often iterative and too slow for practical use. Achieving this with a one-step flow-map model is difficult because both data-distribution and physics-constraint learning are enforced only indirectly through surrogate objectives, making their joint learning fragile for long-range flow maps. We propose \textbf{Physics-Informed Long-Short Flow Maps with Direct Guidance (LSFM-DG)}, which makes direct target computation feasible by decomposing one-step generation into a long flow map for global evolution and a near-terminal short flow map with computable closed-form solutions and physics potentials. As the short flow map shrinks to zero, it provides both direct distributional and physical targets for learning the long flow map. This yields a unified one-step flow-map model that removes Jensen's gap, improves training stability, and achieves stronger data fidelity and physical consistency without distillation.

To better equip the model with multi-step reasoning capability, we further develop PointCoT, a large-scale 3D-specific CoT instruction tuning dataset with approximately 176.2K instances spanning captioning, question answering, dialogue, and planning tasks. Extensive experiments on six benchmarks show that our method consistently improves performance and achieves state-of-the-art results, validating the effectiveness of our proposed ambiguity-aware 3D-text interleaved reasoning for 3D scene understanding.

Title: Wave Physics as Representation: Tensor-Structured Neural Reconstruction for Ocean Acoustic Fields

  • Authors: Yifan Sun, Panqi Chen, Weifeng Shi, Xiao Fu, Lei Cheng
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.SD, eess.AS
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01680
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01680
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01680]] Wave Physics as Representation: Tensor-Structured Neural Reconstruction for Ocean Acoustic Fields(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01680)
  • Keywords: robust, interpretability
  • Abstract: Reconstructing three-dimensional (3D) complex ocean acoustic fields from sparse measurements is challenging due to highly oscillatory wave structures and restricted sensing patterns/routes. Learning-based approaches fall into two categories. First, purely data-driven models can learn complex field patterns, but do not explicitly exploit wave physics that may enhance interpretability and reduce training costs. Second, physics-informed methods impose a specified physical equation as a reconstruction constraint, but often require environment-dependent parameters that are difficult to acquire. As a result, practical 3D acoustic field reconstruction that is both physically grounded and robust to uncertain environmental information remains largely unexplored. This work takes an alternative route: instead of solving a fully specified wave equation, it extracts a structured representation from wave physics and learns its components from data. From the Helmholtz equation and normal mode propagation, we derive a structured spatial-angular tensor representation, termed the S/c representation. Under a low-rank specialization, it takes the form of a rank-(L,L,1) block-term tensor decomposition, which connects structured sampling patterns/routes used in ocean sensing to sufficient conditions for field recovery. Building on this tensor representation, we propose TIDAL, a neural reconstructor that uses the S/c representation as an inductive bias for structured decoding and aligns information aggregation with the sampling geometry. The neural learner removes the need for known model parameters required by conventional physics-based recovery methods. Experiments across three structured sampling geometries show that TIDAL achieves the best reconstruction performance and fastest inference among baselines, with benefits from the physics-derived representation under limited data and model capacity.

Title: MatMoE: Matryoshka Mixture-of-Experts with Dynamic Mixed-Precision Quantization for Efficient Inference

  • Authors: Renjie Wei, Haochen Huang, Chuyu Qiu, Jinqi Wen, Dehao Xu, Meng Li

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture has been widely adopted in large language models (LLMs) since it reduces the computational cost through sparse expert activation. However, the massive parameter scale of MoE models hinders their deployment on resource-constrained edge devices. Although existing mixed-precision quantization reduces the model size, they are inherently static, fixing the bit-width of each expert across decoding steps. Such static schemes yield suboptimal performance as they overlook the dynamic variation in expert importance during decoding. Furthermore, they fail to adapt to the dynamic latency constraint driven by the fluctuating system utilization. To address these challenges, we propose MatMoE, a dynamic mixed-precision quantization framework for efficient MoE inference. MatMoE features: (1) a Matryoshka MoE PTQ algorithm that enables experts to operate flexibly at multiple precisions within a stringent memory budget; (2) a floor-and-compensation quantization method that eliminates redundant memory accesses in the Matryoshka representation; and (3) a dynamic bit-width allocation strategy that determines the precision for each expert based on their routing scores to satisfy the dynamic latency requirement. We implement our MatMoE system atop this http URL for both CPU and GPU platforms. Extensive evaluations across three modern MoE models and challenging benchmarks demonstrate that MatMoE achieves $4.0\% \sim 18.3\%$ higher accuracy than state-of-the-art static mixed-precision method. Compared to this http URL, MatMoE delivers $1.3\times \sim 3.7\times$ speedup on Raspberry Pi 5, and $1.2\times \sim 1.3\times$ speedup on NVIDIA RTX 4090 and Jetson AGX Orin.

It selects a compact context of facts yielding an answer, and derives it; a derivability check tests whether an answer follows, a consistency check whether facts conflict, and a validation-chosen routing gate decides where the derived answer replaces the LLM's. Seven open LLMs read the compact context better in exact match than relevance-ranked facts; on ComplexWebQuestions (CWQ) they match SubgraphRAG's facts at a quarter of the prompt tokens, and stronger LLMs read SubgraphRAG's better. Where the routing gate passes, ours leads SubgraphRAG's in exact match in all 53 LLM, benchmark and prompt settings. A precision gate, returning the derived answer only when the check passes with exactly one derived entity, answered 18 and 5 percent of held-out WebQSP and CWQ questions in a pre-specified test, right for 90 and 96 percent. The consistency check judges facts by path evidence only where another path joins their entities, true of 2.5 percent of named-entity facts in our compact WebQSP and CWQ contexts.

Title: Spectral Signatures of Memorization Basins in Diffusion Trajectories

  • Authors: Achref Jaziri, Martin Mundt, Ahmed Imtiaz Humayun, Visvanthan Ramesh
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01775
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01775
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01775]] Spectral Signatures of Memorization Basins in Diffusion Trajectories(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01775)
  • Keywords: privacy, diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion models are known to reproduce individual examples from their training data as a form of memorization with implications for generalization, privacy, and downstream use. Recent work demonstrated that memorized training samples reside inside contiguous regions of the data/latent space known as basins of memorization -- once a denoising trajectory enters a basin, under finite perturbations it irreversibly reproduces a memorized example. Prior empirical work on such basins in trained models has primarily focused on classifier-free guidance based detection and mitigation strategies in text-to-image models, leaving basin entry and their formation largely uncharacterized. In this paper, we present a geometric characterization of memorization basins for conditional, unconditional, and rectified-flow-based diffusion models. We observe a characteristic signature in the local spectrum of the function geometry that distinguishes basin entry: the emergence of a dominant singular mode followed by a sharp spectral recovery. This signature appears at two timescales: along denoising trajectories during inference and over the course of training. We further investigate whether these geometric signatures can be used to intervene on memorization. During training, we suppress memorization by acting on the dominant mode. At inference, we develop methods to identify trajectories entering memorization basins and steer them away. We find that these interventions remain effective even with low-dimensional approximations of the underlying geometry, and demonstrate that geometry-informed steering extends to large-scale text-to-image diffusion models. Together, our results corroborate that local spectral geometry provides a common basis for characterizing memorization and for suppressing, detecting, and steering away from it across training and generation.

Title: RIFT: Residual-Informed Flow Transfer for One-Step Generative Video Compression

  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01791
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01791
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01791]] RIFT: Residual-Informed Flow Transfer for One-Step Generative Video Compression(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01791)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Recent advances in video generation have enabled generative video compression to reconstruct details lost during coding using pretrained spatiotemporal priors. However, a substantial gap remains between codec-induced degradation and the perturbation trajectories learned by flow-matching models. In this paper, we identify a compression-dependent directional mismatch that degradation-strength-based timestep calibration alone cannot resolve. Compressed latents remain strongly aligned across codec operating points, while their reconstruction residuals vary substantially in magnitude and direction. To bridge this gap, we propose RIFT, a Residual-Informed Flow Transfer framework for one-step generative video compression. At its core, RIFT introduces a Residual Geometry Inducer (RGI) that converts the pretrained flow prediction into a content-adaptive restoration update using decoder-visible codec cues. RGI integrates flow-timestep conditioning with a learned full-band residual-strength estimate and content-conditioned direction and gain modulation, aligning the orientation and magnitude of the update with the compression-dependent residual geometry. Extensive experiments on three public video compression benchmarks show that RIFT outperforms competing generative video compression methods. The code is publicly available on RIFT.

  • Abstract: Email spam and phishing remain persistent threats, and purely algorithmic classifiers may struggle with unfamiliar senders, evolving message content, and cold-start domains.   • Abstract: Facial landmark detection has advanced substantially under regular imaging conditions, yet occlusion, poor illumination, and large pose changes remain major sources of error because local facial evidence can become incomplete or misleading. However, the recovered context can itself be unreliable when it is weakly adapted to the input face, influenced by corrupted neighboring patches, or blended without preserving local spatial coherence. We propose RCFormer, a Reliability-aware Context Transformer for occlusion-robust facial landmark detection. Title: Z-Cache: Accelerating Diffusion Transformers via Self-Reflection

  • Authors: Zegang Cheng, Zhikai Wang, Jiacheng Liu, Xiaobing Tu, Chang Zou, Junjie Chen, Yue Ma, Zhiyuan Ma, Jinkui Ren, Likai Zou, Linfeng Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01846
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01846
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01846]] Z-Cache: Accelerating Diffusion Transformers via Self-Reflection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01846)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Diffusion transformers have become the most powerful models for visual generation, but still suffer from massive computation costs. To solve this problem, feature caching has been proposed to cache the features of diffusion models in the previous computation steps and then reuse them in the following caching steps, which brings significant acceleration but also degradation in generation quality. To address this problem, this paper proposes Z-cache as a feature caching method that can maintain high-quality generation through self-reflection. Concretely, we observe that the error from feature caching tends to be sharply reduced after each full computation. Based on this observation, Z-Cache is designed to first predict the features in the future caching steps and then perform a full computation. After that, Z-Cache returns to the caching steps and re-predicts them based on the previous and the current computation steps, which brings correction in features. Experiments demonstrate that with Z-Cache, diffusion transformers achieve comparable generation quality to the original model but with faster inference speed, for instance, 6.22X acceleration on FLUX-dev for text-to-image generation. Our code is released on GitHub.

  • Abstract: Autoregressive video generation has achieved impressive results on short clips, yet generating long videos remains challenging due to error accumulation over extended horizons, where each predicted frame depends on potentially imperfect previous frames. Existing approaches typically rely on static context management strategies, such as sliding-window caches that discard early frames or fixed sink frames that permanently preserve initial content. These designs either lose critical long-range context or anchor generation to outdated information, which can lead to degraded fidelity, motion stagnation, and reduced diversity. In this paper, we propose Learning to Memorize (L2M), a learned context memory management framework that dynamically retains, evicts, and evolves historical frames according to predicted importance. Trained with a teacher-student distillation objective and sparsity regularization, L2M makes efficient use of a fixed KV-cache budget while preserving the most informative contextual information. Extensive experiments demonstrate that L2M improves long-horizon video generation quality and temporal consistency over existing baselines, providing a learned strategy for context memory management in long autoregressive video generation.

Title: Quantizing Recurrent Language Models Is Easier Than You Think

  • Authors: Philipp Nazari, Neehal Tumma, Daniela Rus, T. Konstantin Rusch
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01864
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01864
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01864]] Quantizing Recurrent Language Models Is Easier Than You Think(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01864)
  • Keywords: protect, robust, transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Linear recurrences are becoming an increasingly common building block in large language models, replacing the growing KV cache of softmax attention with a fixed-size recurrent state that enables efficient decoding. However, the quantization stack has largely been developed around softmax Transformers, whose numerical properties and inference state differ from those of recurrent models. Our goal is to understand these differences and study the design space of quantization methods for recurrent models. In our analysis, we differentiate between two targets. First, extra-state quantization concerns weights and activations outside the recurrent state, whose distributions are shaped indirectly by the sequence mixer during training. In controlled 1.3B parameter models, we find that decayed recurrences develop weaker residual outlier channels than softmax attention and are therefore easier to quantize without incoherence processing. Second, intra-state quantization concerns the recurrent state itself during decode, where repeated updates create a different numerical problem from KV cache quantization. We instantiate our findings as LoReS, a state quantization pipeline combining value-axis scale grouping to match output channel magnitude, decay-based mixed-precision to protect slowly decaying heads or channels, and stochastic rounding to limit error accumulation under repeated requantization. Across Qwen-3.5 9B, Nemotron-H 8B, and Kimi-Linear 48B-A3B, LoReS supports robust 8-bit state quantization and lower effective precision on shorter decode horizons. We implement LoReS in vLLM and demonstrate end-to-end decode speedups.

  • Abstract: Head motion causes artefacts in 3D brain MRI and can bias structural measurements.   • Authors: Jiacheng Lu, Qixuan Yang, Tao Song, Daxin Jiang, Jing Li, Zuo Bai, Haibing Guan

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Coding-agent services are priced by the model they claim, making the verification of such model claims crucial for their users. This assumption does not hold for coding-agent services, whose providers may legitimately change the sampling preset, the reasoning mode or the system prompt without changing the model. Extensive experiments on eight claims, two agent harnesses and three task suites verify the effectiveness of CertClaim, which is valid in every setting while separating substitutes far better than existing methods, as well as its resistance to potential adaptive attacks.

Title: Step-Zero Attack: Agent Safety Before Observability

  • Authors: Tian Qin, Yuhang Jiang, Junzhe Chen, Lit Sin Tan, Lijie Wen
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01953
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01953
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01953]] Step-Zero Attack: Agent Safety Before Observability(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01953)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, extraction
  • Abstract: Existing attacks and defenses for coding agents act once content enters the model context: injected text persuades the model, and guards inspect what the model reads. Yet to judge whether processing an unknown archive, network response, or image is safe, an agent often must process it first, and that step can do harm before any content reaches the model. We call this first processing Step Zero, and harm done by it a Step-Zero attack. Across seven harnesses and six models, each agent received only a path or URL to a crafted input, paired with a benign twin showing the same visible fields. Step Zero is where most agents are already unsafe: 32/42 harness-model pairs overran a 4 MiB disk budget, 26/42 before the model saw any member, and four of the six models did so in every round (84/84), so the damage precedes any model-side defense. Every twin stayed within budget, so the attacker's bytes alone cause the harm. On network and image inputs the risk moves to what the agent writes: agents stored the body under the name the input declared, letting the input decide what is written, and their checks faded once cues were removed. Model-free tests trace the harm to the processing primitive, so we defend it there: a gate that counts the bytes each call produces held every routed extraction within budget while agents completed 25/28 tasks. Vendors should pair such gates with safety-alignment training that teaches agents to inspect an input before processing it.

Title: Object-Only Is Not Context-Free: A Counterfactual Diagnostic for Context Removal in Object Retrieval

  • Authors: Boaz Meivar, Shai Avidan
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01962
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01962
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.01962]] Object-Only Is Not Context-Free: A Counterfactual Diagnostic for Context Removal in Object Retrieval(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01962)
  • Keywords: transformer, segmentation
  • Abstract: Object-centric retrieval pipelines often mask the background before pooling backbone features, aiming for a descriptor that reflects the object rather than its scene. Whether this truly yields an object-only descriptor, however, is rarely tested. We introduce the Keyhole Aperture Probe (KAP), a counterfactual diagnostic that repurposes the ground-truth masks of existing panoptic segmentation datasets (COCO, ADE20K) for retrieval: it holds the pooled object region fixed and varies only the pixels outside it, summarizing a backbone's context dependence in a single number. The dependence is also reducible: a lightweight adapter that encourages the descriptor to stay stable as context is removed lowers it and improves full-image instance retrieval on INSTRE, transferring to ILIAS with accuracy improved or preserved. A matched contrastive-fine-tuning control does not reproduce those gains. We release KAP as a simple, backbone-agnostic evaluation protocol to measure and reduce this dependence before deployment.

We further examine existing defenses and find that they provide insufficient protection against this threat. These findings expose a fundamental blind spot in current agent security practices: trust established for a tool does not necessarily remain valid after its implementation updates.

Title: MIRA: Mutual Information guided calibration for Reliable Test-Time Adaptation

  • Authors: Hyeongyu Kim, Mingyeong Jang, Youngjun Song, GeonHui Han, Dosik Hwang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02003
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02003
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.02003]] MIRA: Mutual Information guided calibration for Reliable Test-Time Adaptation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02003)
  • Keywords: robust, extraction
  • Abstract: Test-time adaptation (TTA) updates a deployed model using only unlabeled target data, but standard entropy-based methods often rely on deterministic confidence and can reinforce overconfident errors under distribution shift. We propose MIRA, Mutual Information guided calibration for Reliable Test-Time Adaptation, a source-free online adaptation method that uses lightweight last-layer Gaussian perturbations to probe prediction reliability. For each target batch, MIRA reuses a single feature extraction pass and samples perturbed classifiers around the source classifier, forming an efficient local predictive distribution. Rather than fitting a Bayesian posterior, MIRA treats the perturbation scale as a local probing radius and selects a batch-specific scale using mutual information (MI) and prediction agreement. The resulting perturbation-sensitivity signal identifies predictions that are confident and locally stable under classifier perturbations, while suppressing brittle predictions with high stochastic disagreement. MIRA then performs adaptation with an MI-confidence weighted entropy loss, which down-weights perturbation-sensitive or low-confidence samples before they drive entropy minimization. This weighted loss reduces the influence of brittle predictions that can otherwise cause harmful adaptation under distribution shift. The method requires no source data, retraining, model ensembles, or offline posterior fitting, and can be applied as a plug-and-play modification to entropy-minimization TTA. Experiments on distribution-shift benchmarks show improvements in accuracy, calibration, and negative log-likelihood, suggesting that mutual-information-guided perturbation probing and weighted adaptation yield more robust online TTA than deterministic confidence.

Title: Scalar Smoothness Is One Point on a Besov Spectrum

  • Authors: Bum Jun Kim, Anastasis Kratsios
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02037
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02037

  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Studies of neural network generalization and robustness often summarize smoothness with a single number, such as a Lipschitz constant or a Hessian curvature statistic. However, Lipschitz constants summarize global worst-case sensitivity and Hessian statistics summarize local curvature; neither captures how model responses vary with the size, direction, and location of perturbations encountered in real-world deployment. To measure smoothness under these perturbations, we introduce a finite-difference roughness spectrum that profiles model responses across these conditions. Our roughness spectrum generalizes scalar smoothness measures, bringing Lipschitz sensitivity and directional Hessian curvature into a common framework across perturbation scales. We connect this spectrum to classical Besov theory, which describes how a function's variation changes with scale. Our theoretical analysis also proves that a larger output shift can cause less task damage than a smaller one, and that local curvature alone can miss the blocks most vulnerable to finite perturbations. In image classification, Roughness Source Control (RSC) suppresses margin roughness along noise directions and improves noise robustness in a comparison of 80 checkpoints across four model--dataset combinations. In post-training quantization, we identify blocks that need higher precision by measuring task-loss increases from perturbations matched to the size and direction of each block's quantization error. Our mixed-precision allocation achieves lower perplexity than the Hessian-based HAWQ-V2 baseline under the same GPTQ weights and bit budgets in all 14 settings across seven held-out large language models.

Title: SANAI: Secure ECC Distributed Keyed Parity Embedded in Weights for Protecting Neural Networks against Bit-Flip Attacks

  • Authors: Habibur Rahaman, Daniel Lobo, Md Rezoan Ferdous, Swarup Bhunia
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02042
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02042
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.02042]] SANAI: Secure ECC Distributed Keyed Parity Embedded in Weights for Protecting Neural Networks against Bit-Flip Attacks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02042)
  • Keywords: secure, protect, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Quantized neural networks store weights in dense memory, where bit flips caused by voltage scaling, aging, radiation, or deliberate attacks such as RowHammer can severely degrade model behavior. A single corrupted high-order bit can cause misclassifications or nonsensical language-model outputs. Embedded error-correcting codes (ECC) avoid dedicated parity storage by hiding parity within model weights, but existing schemes embed uniformly across layers and bit planes, leave parity unsecured, and tolerate bit-error rates (BERs) only up to about 6%. An adversary aware of sensitive weights and the ECC scheme can also bypass protection by exceeding the correction capability of targeted codewords. We propose SANAI, a sensitivity-guided, bit-plane-selective scheme that protects critical model weights against bit flips with zero additional parity-storage overhead. SANAI secures parity through keyed embedding across a two-dimensional weight space: spread-transform dither modulation binds parity to a secret key, and keyed interleaving disperses concentrated attacks across codewords. We derive a survival law showing that robustness is governed by the correctable fraction t/n rather than by t alone, and a closed-form bound on the protectable fraction of a model. As a streaming checker on an 8x8 systolic array, SANAI verifies weights on the load path with 0.41% area overhead and no additional memory. Across ten vision backbones, three quantization schemes, and ten language models from 82M to 13B parameters, SANAI stays within about one percentage point of clean accuracy at BERs up to 12%, twice the maximum of prior embedded-ECC approaches. It survives fine-tuning bit-exactly, neutralizes progressive and targeted bit-flip attacks, and exactly corrects AttentionBreaker and SilentStriker attacks.

Title: Beliefs in feedback: Factored representations of interacting agents in transformers

  • Authors: David A. Ehrlich, Manuel Baltieri, Fernando E. Rosas, Alexander B. Boyd
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.MA, nlin.AO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02047
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02047
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.02047]] Beliefs in feedback: Factored representations of interacting agents in transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02047)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Complex systems, from ecosystems to brains, are usually modeled as modular components coupled through feedback rather than as indivisible wholes. Can a transformer trained on the joint behavior of a group of interacting agents be understood in the same way? In this work, we develop a computational-mechanics framework to study agents interacting in feedback loops. We formally prove that any joint stochastic process can be presented as resulting from the behavior of agents interacting in feedback, and the resulting process is generated by a hidden Markov model whose Bayesian beliefs factorize into separate components per agent. Furthermore, our experiments show that transformers trained on such feedback sequences make use of this separability, disentangling the beliefs about the underlying factors into separate subspaces of their residual stream. This representation is found to scale linearly (instead of exponentially) with the number of agents. Moreover, interventions show that these representations are causally efficacious, allowing for targeted steering of individual agents' beliefs by selectively inserting counterfactuals. However, coarse-graining the behavior is found to disrupt the factorization, and can force a higher-dimensional representation. Together, the results establish both the dimensional advantage of a factorized representation and the ability of transformers to exploit it, laying the groundwork for investigating traces of compositional predictive architecture in pretrained models.

  • Authors: Mohammad Reza Rezaei, Dingxian Wang, Nikhil Verma, Sajjad Pakdamansavoji, Andrew Rabinovich, Zhao Chen
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02065
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02065
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.02065]] Fragility Is Not Reducible to Hardness: Conditional Causal Difficulty for LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02065)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models show large and seemingly arbitrary performance drops when a task is rewritten in ways that leave its requirements untouched. We recast task difficulty as a causal quantity: the treatment effect of meaning-preserving perturbations on a model. A meaning-preserving rewrite can only be observed to hurt a task the model can already solve, so we place the estimand on the principal stratum where the degradation occurs: the conditional causal difficulty (CCD), the probability that a randomly drawn twin breaks a solvable task. Because harder tasks tend to be more phrasing-sensitive, complexity enters as an effect modifier rather than a confounder, and we residualize it out to obtain complexity-residualized difficulty (CRD); zero for a task exactly as fragile as its complexity predicts, positive for anomalously fragile ones. Current benchmarks do not supply the data CCD requires, so we introduce GlassGSM and GlassMATH, augmenting GSM8K and MATH with twins built from perturbation families, raising fragility on GlassGSM by $\approx 12\times$ over GSM-IC-style distractors on the same problems ($3.7\%\to44.9\%$) while keeping the answer key unchanged. Our predictor Frail learns the per-perturbation break probability and reaches $0.613{\pm}0.023$ of the suite reliability ceiling on GlassGSM and $0.557{\pm}0.023$ on GlassMATH, against $0.171$/$0.401$ for a surface-feature baseline and $0.444$/$0.463$ for supervised baselines such as VAP3, and, at a $5\%$ curation budget on GlassGSM, doubles random recall of fragile items, matching an oracle ranking. Additionally, cross-fitted residualization leaves CRD uncorrelated with the declared proxy in practice. Code is available at this https URL

Title: Scene-Adaptive Readout and Decoding in Diffusion Driving VLAs

  • Authors: Seungjun Yu, Jiho Choi, Inha Kang, Hojun Choi, Seungwook Kim, Hyunjung Shim
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02076
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02076
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.02076]] Scene-Adaptive Readout and Decoding in Diffusion Driving VLAs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02076)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion driving vision-language-action models repeatedly execute the backbone at every denoising stage, incurring substantial inference cost, while it remains unclear whether all scenes require the same decoding computation. To distinguish when a trajectory becomes readable from how much further refinement it requires, we analyze trajectory readout across network depth and decoding progress. We identify a depth-decoding dissociation in which late-layer representations already support useful trajectory readout after the first forward sweep, whereas early-layer representations benefit more from continued decoding. We further find that both the preferred readout layer and decoding depth vary across scenes. Based on these observations, we propose a scene-adaptive readout and decoding framework. A scene-conditioned readout MoE adaptively weights and combines trajectory predictions from layer-specific late-layer representations after a single backbone sweep, while an adaptive decoding policy applies additional native refinement only when required by the scene. We evaluate our framework on two diffusion driving VLAs across the Waymo E2E and nuScenes benchmarks. Our method improves validation RFS by up to 0.56 while also improving test performance, and achieves an end-to-end speedup of up to $6.0\times$ on Waymo E2E, while consistently reducing trajectory error on nuScenes. These results demonstrate that scene-level readout and decoding allocation improves planning quality while avoiding unnecessary decoding. Our code is available at this https URL

Title: HQNN-CL: Regime-Aware Hybrid Quantum Neural Network with Continual Learning for Financial Forecasting

  • Authors: Muhammad Atif Saeed, Akhtar Jamil, Subhan Ullah
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02091
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02091

  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: Financial time-series forecasting is still difficult owing to the non-stationary nature of the market, regime changes, and catastrophic forgetting, which makes conventional deep learning models hard to adapt in the long term. To achieve uncertainty-aware robust financial forecasting in the face of changing market conditions, this study proposes a Hybrid Quantum-Classical Neural Network with Continual Learning (HQNN-CL). The proposed framework combines Gaussian Hidden Markov Model (HMM) based regime detection, BiLSTM temporal encoding, adaptive-depth parameterized quantum circuits, Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC), experience replay, and probabilistic forecasting in a single architecture. Ablation and statistical significance analyses also validate the performance of adaptive quantum feature learning and continual adaptation under heterogeneous market conditions, regime-wise.

  • Abstract: Vision-language models for remote sensing answer questions about satellite images by generating text: every answer costs a decoding loop, has to be parsed back to an option, and comes without a usable probability. Replacing the nine non-RGB bands with their mean costs 4.5 points, so the model uses the multispectral input, and in an ablation training the whole vision encoder raises accuracy to 88.4% at the same cost. Title: Consolidating Reasoning with Test-Time Training

  • Authors: Haotian Wu, Zeming Chen, Hao Zhao, Antoine Bosselut
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02104
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02104
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.02104]] Consolidating Reasoning with Test-Time Training(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02104)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Effective multi-agent collaboration requires finalizing solutions from reasoning that is distributed across the individual attempts of many agents. However, useful intermediate reasoning can remain unexploited when collaboration is limited to voting on conclusions reached among individual agents, or aggregating long, noisy collections of reasoning trajectories in context. In this work, we propose TTT-Consolidate, a framework that uses multi-agent reasoning trajectories as adaptation data to improve a model's subsequent reasoning. TTT-Consolidate jointly consolidates trajectories into parametric representations through test-time training, then generates a new solution by conditioning on the consolidated information. We meta-learn how to consolidate using bi-level optimization at training time, where an inner loop encodes reasoning trajectories into a lightweight LoRA adapter, and an outer loop learns meta-parameters for the inner loop such that the consolidation is helpful for the model to synthesize a final solution. As no large-scale corpus of diverse reasoning trajectories exists for training aggregation methods, we construct the OpenParallelThinking corpus composed of 12,810 math problems paired with a mixture of trajectories from six LLMs. Evaluated on challenging math and STEM benchmarks, TTT-Consolidate outperforms the best aggregation baselines by up to 20 percentage points, even under matched compute and runtime settings, with the gap widening at larger model scales. TTT-Consolidate also recovers from unsuccessful individual trajectories more robustly, an ability that improves as the number of available (even incorrect) trajectories increases. Finally, we demonstrate that TTT-Consolidate benefits from both SFT and RL-based outer loop objectives. We will release TTT-Consolidate and OpenParallelThinking for use in future work on reasoning aggregation.

Title: ZT-DAC: ML-Driven Dynamic Access Control Policy for Zero Trust in DER Networks

  • Authors: Arun B. Chandrasekaran, Damilola Popoola, Souradeep Bhattacharya, Manimaran Govindarasu
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02107

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: The increasing penetration of Distributed Energy Resources (DERs) in the smart grid has significantly expanded the cyber-attack surface. Traditionally, DER networks rely on static role-based access control (RBAC) mechanisms that assume persistent trust after authentication. This limitation enables adversaries to maintain unauthorized privileges and move laterally across DER systems, disrupting grid operations. Although attribute-based access control (ABAC) enables fine-grained and context-aware access decisions, it does not fully eliminate implicit trust. Zero Trust (ZT) principles can be incorporated to enhance ABAC policies. This paper proposes a dynamic access control framework for implementing ZT in DER networks (ZT-DAC).   • Authors: Li-Ming Yu

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Standard attention forms query (Q), key (K), and value (V) matrices through learned linear projections of input hidden states. Architectures such as GAU and Mega incorporate nonlinear transformations into attention. In this paper, we propose GatedV, a simple attention variant that applies SiLU only to V before aggregation without introducing additional trainable parameters. We evaluate GatedV against a standard decoder-only Transformer using matched parameter counts, paired initializations, and equal training-token budgets. At 2.33M parameters, GatedV reduces mean held-out perplexity from 59.1 to 57.9 (2.0%), with improvements across all five paired seeds. Small-model ablations show that the tested affine rescaling of values does not fully reproduce GatedV's gains. A short interleaved benchmark on NVIDIA DGX Spark measures approximately 0.7% higher full-context forward latency for the larger model, based on synchronized wall-clock timings. These findings suggest that value-only self-gating can improve language modeling performance without increasing parameter count or redesigning the attention-weighting mechanism.

Title: When Is Personalized Federated Learning Worth Its Deployment Cost?

  • Authors: Beyazit Bestami Yuksel, Ayse Yilmazer Metin, Emrah Dikbiyik
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02146
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02146
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.02146]] When Is Personalized Federated Learning Worth Its Deployment Cost?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02146)
  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Personalized federated learning (PFL) may improve client-level performance, but deeper personalization increases deployment cost. We formulate personalization-depth selection as a utility-based empirical decision problem in which client utility equals Macro-F1 minus a cost-sensitivity weight times deployment cost, over six personalization depths labeled P0 through P5. We compare fixed policies with an offline per-client Oracle reference and report allocation maps over Dirichlet data heterogeneity and cost sensitivity. Although evaluated on this hierarchy, the formulation can accommodate any finite candidate set. On CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and a PTB-XL subset, personalization shows empirical, setting-dependent decision regimes rather than a universal benefit: higher cost sensitivity shifts Oracle mass toward the global-only depth P0; low cost sensitivity can favor deeper actions. Patterns are grid observations (three seeds per cell), not theoretical boundaries. Best-uniform is competitive with Oracle assignment (6.5 percent plus or minus 7.8 percent relative regret; 95 percent confidence interval 5.9 to 7.1), whereas random and personalize-all incur much larger regret (189.3 percent plus or minus 367.1 percent and 409.0 percent plus or minus 803.2 percent). Under the same utility, Ditto full-model personalization is substantially suboptimal once cost is counted. The contribution is an offline characterization of when personalization depth is worth its cost, not a new PFL optimizer or deployment-time scheduler.

Title: Multi-Agent Collusion and the J-Space

  • Authors: Marco Molinari, Gabriele Bettineschi
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02153
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02153

  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly deployed in Multi-Agent Systems (MAS), and they introduce the risk of coordination among agents that might escape human oversight. The J-space has been used to uncover deception in single LLMs, but its use for uncovering and steering collusion between agents remains unexplored. We show, with interpretability experiments on DeepSeek-V4-Flash (284B) on the NARCBench collusion benchmark, that colluding agents' J-space holds the collusion itself: before replying, 86% of colluders represent misrepresentation, concealment or remorse. We also introduce the Jacobian Pair Contrast (J-PACT), an unsupervised method for detecting collusion between agents. We evaluate J-PACT on four open-weight models: GPT-OSS-20B, Llama-3.3-70B-IT, Qwen3-32B, and DeepSeek-V4-Flash (284B). J-PACT retains 85% of the above-chance AUROC of supervised NARCBench probes, matches them on steganographic scenarios, and names the colluding pair up to its complement in 99% of detected runs.

  • Abstract: Machine learning models have shown exceptional accuracy for ballot classification in election systems. In addition to digital analyses, we physically print and scan $20,000$ adversarial examples to evaluate the real-world performance of different black-box attacks. Our finding demonstrate an even larger physical-digital robustness gap exists for black-box adversarial examples than for white-box adversarial examples. Lastly, we conduct the first ever adversarial example transferability study in the voting domain and test adversarial training and barrier zones as two possible black-box defense strategies.

  • Subjects: cs.LG, physics.chem-ph
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02170

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.02170]] Restrained Diffusion Bridges: Targeted Denoiser Training and Refinement(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02170)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion models are often inaccurate in critical, data-sparse regions of the target distribution. Sampling from restrained distributions, biased distributions used in classical enhanced sampling to concentrate data near chosen configurations, can control where training data are collected but biases the learned distribution. We establish that this bias cancels exactly when forward noising from restrained data is conditioned on the restrained configuration at an appropriately chosen intermediate time. This allows a pretrained model fine-tuned on restrained data to achieve high accuracy in any chosen region of the target distribution, regardless of its probability, using standard denoising or flow matching objectives without importance weights. When no target data are available, Gibbs sampling enables inference from a model trained exclusively on restrained data.

Title: A Matched Evaluation of QUBO Action Models and Defensive Remediation in Cyber Attack Graphs

  • Authors: Kasymkhan Khubiev, Yaroslav Kholodov, Alexey S. Petrenko
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02179
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02179

  • Keywords: protect, attack
  • Abstract: This study examines how vulnerability-specific actions affect quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) models for minimum-cost cyber attack paths and defensive remediation. Edge-flow and variable-length hop formulations are evaluated using simulated annealing, steepest descent, and tabu search against exact classical references. Experiments on six independent synthetic topologies separate the effects of transition eligibility, vulnerability labels, exploit costs, and multiple exploit alternatives. A single labeled action per eligible transition preserves feasible paths, optimal costs, and QUBO size. Under the tested settings, multiple exploit alternatives were associated with approximately unchanged valid-path recovery, lower optimal-path recovery, and substantially larger representations, particularly for the hop formulation. These recovery differences remain descriptive because they are not statistically significant after correction for multiple comparisons. An evaluation on a published 3CX attack graph with synthetic costs demonstrates applicability beyond the generated topologies. The defensive extension selects patches within a budget to raise attack-path costs to specified thresholds or disconnect protected targets. Against exhaustive and mixed-integer references, the tested QUBO heuristics recovered an optimal patch set in 12 of 16 calls across eight scenarios on one topology. The results characterize formulation correctness, solution recovery, and resource requirements using classical local optimization, without establishing quantum computational advantage.

Title: Forecasting Earth Surface Change in Embedding Space

  • Authors: Jingtao Li, Sadiq Jaffer, Anil Madhavapeddy, Pedro Sousa, Anthony Ortiz, Kyle Gao, Zhengpeng Feng, Yanfei Zhong, Kevin White, Srinivasan Keshav
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02211
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02211
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.02211]] Forecasting Earth Surface Change in Embedding Space(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02211)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Repeated satellite imagery permits understanding of how our planet changes over time, letting us predict its future and plan mitigating actions. Most existing studies that rely on a time series of satellite observations forecast the spectral values at each pixel for a future target date. However, clouds, shadows, and sensor noise often corrupt these observations, so that spectral forecasters waste much of their capacity on low-level features or noise rather than real change. With the advent of Earth foundation models, we argue that forecasting in embedding space is a feasible and more effective alternative. To this end, we design a token space that handles irregular histories from different sensors and foundation models and supports forecasts at any query date. Tokens from this space are inputs to our proposed forecaster, E$^2$Forecast, which is a transformer with two branches: a Gaussian branch that directly predicts a mean point forecast and per-dimension standard deviations, and a rectified-flow branch that generates forecast ensembles. On Sentinel-1 and Sentinel-2 benchmarks, forecasting embeddings instead of spectral values halves the cosine distance (from 0.20 to 0.10) and greatly reduces overconfidence, lowering the z-score standard deviation from 2.63 to 1.22. By comparing new observations with its forecasts, E$^2$Forecast can also detect anomalous changes without labels or retraining, raising F$_1$ from 41.58 to 48.70 on a global benchmark. This consistent superiority is validated on nine remote sensing foundation models. Beyond change detection, we believe our approach advances the field by permitting effective reuse of diverse pretrained task heads. Our code is publicly available at this https URL.

Title: Metamorphic Testing for Evaluating Robustness of Sentiment Analysis Models

  • Authors: Sowjanya Karanam, Jayanth Bhargav
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02248

  • Keywords: robust, generative
  • Abstract: Machine learning systems are challenging to test using traditional software testing methods because their behavior is learned from data rather than explicitly programmed. This challenge is particularly significant in natural language processing (NLP), where small input variations can lead to unexpected model behavior. Conventional evaluation metrics, such as accuracy and F1-score, often fail to capture such inconsistencies. Metamorphic testing addresses this limitation by evaluating expected relationships between original and transformed inputs. With the increasing prevalence of generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) and language-based AI systems, the need for systematic robustness testing has become even more pronounced. Using consistency score, flip accuracy, and confidence change, we identify strengths in handling semantic variations and vulnerabilities to textual noise and logical transformations. These findings highlight the value of metamorphic testing in identifying behavioral inconsistencies beyond conventional performance metrics and demonstrate its potential as a systematic framework for evaluating the robustness of language-based AI systems and guiding performance improvements before deployment.

  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made substantial progress in 3D scene understanding from multi-view RGB observations. Yet a fixed object and query can elicit inconsistent target-related responses across camera viewpoints, a phenomenon we characterize as viewpoint-induced semantic drift. We introduce LieVLM, a Lie routing framework for view-robust 3D understanding. Motivated by the rotational component of viewpoint change, Lie routing adapts visual representations by applying local $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ transformations to projected features within learned three-dimensional subspaces. Geometry tokens extracted by VGGT predict local rotation parameters and channel-wise routing coefficients. Controlled ablations examine the roles of geometric conditioning and transformation structure. Viewpoint analyses further reveal systematic variation in the predicted routing states alongside more localized target responses and stronger task performance across viewpoint changes.

Title: Do Visual RL Gains Scale at Test Time? A Quantile View of Post-Training

  • Authors: Austin Wang, Lexing Ying
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02366
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02366
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.02366]] Do Visual RL Gains Scale at Test Time? A Quantile View of Post-Training(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02366)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training improves the single-sample quality of visual generative models, but deployment often provides additional inference compute through Best-of-N sampling. Whether RL gains transfer under such test-time scaling, particularly on difficult held-out tasks, remains poorly understood in the visual domain. Our central contribution is to systematically identify and characterize a failure mode of scaling transfer in visual RL: across multiple RL methods, model backbones, and evaluations spanning compositional generation, text rendering, and preference alignment, higher single-sample reward does not necessarily imply a better quality-compute frontier. RL gains may attenuate, saturate, or even reverse at large sampling budgets, with the base model eventually outperforming its post-trained counterpart. We explain these regimes through a quantile-transfer framework, showing that Best-of-N places increasing weight on the upper tail of the reward distribution as N grows. Consequently, RL can improve typical samples while compressing or degrading the rare high-quality outcomes that test-time scaling exploits. As a simple intervention motivated by this analysis, we introduce Quantile Utility Policy Optimization (QUPO), which optimizes a controllable mixture of quantile utilities corresponding to different inference budgets. Experiments on SD3.5-M and BAGEL show that QUPO better preserves upper-tail quality and substantially improves scaling on challenging held-out prompts relative to standard RL post-training. Our results suggest that visual generative models should be evaluated and optimized not only for single-sample quality, but for how post-training reshapes the full reward distribution that inference-time scaling exploits.

Title: Solver-Like Behavior in Large Language Models as Formalizers

  • Authors: Rikhil Amonkar, Stuti Mohan, Kevin Nguyen, Ceyhun Efe Kayan, Ronan Le Bras, Li Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02395
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02395
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.02395]] Solver-Like Behavior in Large Language Models as Formalizers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02395)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: In the LLM-as-formalizer paradigm, a large language model translates a problem into a formal program and leaves the search to a solver, gaining the solver's guarantees. Formalizers are now often reasoning models that write a long reasoning trace before the program, yet they are judged only by the program's output. We find that the trace routinely does the solver's job, assigning, testing and ruling out values until it often reaches the full answer; we call this solver-like behavior (SLB). We define an ontology of SLB over the trace, the program and its output, and use LLM judges, checked against human labels, to label 2,000 generations from three reasoning models on four constraint satisfaction datasets, each formalized in a declarative and an imperative formal language. SLB appears in 85% of traces and takes 24% of reasoning tokens, yet the program usually recomputes the answer, so the effort is redundant. The 9% of programs that inherit the trace's answer also inherit its errors, and pass output-based evaluation whenever that answer is correct. The amount of SLB is not associated with accuracy, and SLB is more common in imperative Python than in declarative Z3 or PDDL. Cautionary prompts reduce it by only a quarter, but hiding the problem's values behind placeholders, which the program receives only at run time, removes 97% of it without loss of accuracy. Formalizers should therefore be evaluated by how their programs are reached, not only by what they output.

Title: A Novel Federated Learning Framework for Defending Against Gradient Inversion Attacks

  • Authors: Pengxin Guo, Ziying Huang, Shuang Zeng, Xiaodan Zhang, Weihong Ren, Yuyin Zhou, Liangqiong Qu
  • Subjects: cs.LG

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02412
