Title: Attacking P2P Networks: Liveness Attacks on the GossipSub and DOG Gossip Protocols

  • Authors: Jiaheng Li, Anton Paramonov, Yann Vonlanthen
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03509
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03509
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.03509]] Attacking P2P Networks: Liveness Attacks on the GossipSub and DOG Gossip Protocols(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03509)
  • Keywords: attack, robust
  • Abstract: Peer-to-peer (P2P) networks form the communication layer of large decentralized systems such as Ethereum, and the correctness of higher-level protocols depends on the gossip layer delivering messages to honest nodes. We study the robustness of two gossip protocols against adversaries whose only goal is to violate liveness, that is, to prevent honest nodes from receiving fresh messages, without forging, altering, or invalidating any message. For GossipSub, the publish-subscribe protocol used by Ethereum, we show that an adversary operating a constant number of geographically distributed nodes with a fixed latency advantage can take over the ``First Message Deliveries'' peer-scoring term and separate a constant fraction of nodes from the honest mesh. We analyze the attack under a geographical model, address the random-peer and outbound-connection mechanisms, and show that the separation can be maintained under realistic churn. For DOG, a transaction-dissemination protocol designed for CometBFT, we first give simple counterexamples in which route filtering disconnects the dissemination graph even without failures or Byzantine participants, and then present a practical attack that uses only a single physical node with a constant number of identities and legitimate actions to disable every forwarding route, separating the full network while allowing only direct publisher-to-neighbor delivery. We verify the attack in a simulated network and analyze its cost, assumptions, and possible mitigations.

Title: RASP: Read-All, Sparse-Plasticity Continual Learning in a Stationary-Size LoRA

  • Authors: Wen-Hsuan Liu, Amara Okonkwo, Tom Saliencro, Rohan Desai, Maya Lindqvist
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03513
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03513
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.03513]] RASP: Read-All, Sparse-Plasticity Continual Learning in a Stationary-Size LoRA(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03513)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Parameter-efficient continual learning on pre-trained Vision Transformers largely relies on a costly premise: preventing catastrophic forgetting requires either spawning a dedicated LoRA branch per session---causing adapter parameters to scale linearly with task horizon---or applying sparse Mixture-of-Experts gating symmetrically to both forward and backward passes, which sacrifices representational rank at test time. We argue that reading consolidated knowledge and writing new task credit are fundamentally asymmetric operations. Because visual features in a shared low-rank space distribute across rank-1 atoms rather than localizing in disjoint task silos, forward inference should read all atoms at full rank capacity, whereas backward optimization should restrict plasticity to a sparse subset of input-aligned channels. We introduce RASP (Read-All, Sparse-Plasticity LoRA), which adapts a single stationary-size adapter across sequential tasks. At each step, a scene-modulated cosine router selects the top-$q$ unlocked atoms to receive gradients, blocks updates on the remaining $r-q$ channels, and penalizes Frobenius Gram coherence between active and non-updated output directions. Across sessions, the router's empirical activation trace and each atom's exact spectral norm form a zero-overhead geometric consolidation index $\omega_j = \sqrt{\tilde{c}_j \tilde{\sigma}_j}$ that drives Cauchy step attenuation and boundary anchor locking. Across CIFAR-100, CUB-200, ImageNet-R, and ImageNet-A, RASP matches or surpasses branch-expanding LoRA learners under a strictly constant parameter footprint.

Title: VIAS: Value-Isolating Activation Steering for Multi-Value Alignment and Compliance for Organisations

  • Authors: Avni Mittal, Avinash Anand, Dikshant Kukreja, Erik Cambria, Rajiv Ratn Shah, Sandipan Dandapat

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03518
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03518
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.03518]] VIAS: Value-Isolating Activation Steering for Multi-Value Alignment and Compliance for Organisations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03518)
  • Keywords: protect, large language model
  • Abstract: Organisations increasingly deploy large language model agents that must follow organisational values. We check whether activation steering, which adds vectors to a model's internal activations during generation without changing its weights, can improve compliance beyond prompting alone. Since most scenarios involve several values at once, we focus on multi-value steering. A simple way to build these vectors is to contrast responses that follow all values relevant to a scenario with responses that violate them. However, this gives nearly identical vectors for different values: they capture a general compliance signal rather than individual values, which limits their use when combining several values. We propose Value-Isolating Activation Steering (VIAS), which contrasts responses to the same scenario under two prompts, one listing all relevant values and one omitting a single target value. We keep a pair only if the target value changes from compliant to non-compliant and both responses still meet a minimum compliance level on the other values. We evaluate VIAS on PROTECT, a workplace compliance dataset where each scenario is tagged with relevant values from a 21-value taxonomy. VIAS reduces overlap between value vectors, making them better suited for multi-value steering, although this alone does not lead to independent control over each value. Combined with prompting, VIAS complies with every tagged value on 69.6% of scenarios for Qwen2.5-7B, 75.2% for Mistral-7B, and 72.6% for Phi-3.5-mini, outperforming prompting alone for all three models.

Title: Library of Layers (LoL): Adaptively Ordering Transformer Layers at Runtime

  • Abstract: Computer programmers chain together the same reusable computational primitives, for example, conditional statements or loops, but in different orders to implement different algorithms. In contrast, existing language model architectures always execute their layers in the same order. We introduce Library of Layers (LoL), an architecture that dynamically orders its layers during inference to solve the problem at hand. LoL has a single hyperparameter, the size of its global library of transformer blocks, that allows it to transition seamlessly between parameter reuse and sparsity, generalizing the parameter-efficiency and FLOPs-efficiency tradeoffs of dense, depth-recurrent, and mixture of experts architectures in one unified family. We show that LoL achieves competitive performance with all three above baselines when pretrained on text data. In a multilingual natural language setting, we find that LoL chooses different layer order patterns based on language and script. LoL provides a path toward a future in which models dynamically compose their own computational graph, enabling more efficient and controllable reasoning.

Title: Contrastive Adapter Training and Consensus Knowledge Distillation for Multi-Source-Free Domain Adaptation in Skin Cancer Diagnosis

  • Authors: Ali Abedi, Q. M. Jonathan Wu, Ning Zhang, Farhad Pourpanah

  • Keywords: privacy, transformer
  • Abstract: Skin cancer diagnosis, particularly differentiating melanoma from benign nevi, is challenging due to the visual similarity between lesions. Although convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and vision transformers (ViTs) have shown promising performance, their effectiveness often deteriorates on data from heterogeneous clinical sources. Conventional domain adaptation methods address domain shift but require access to source data during adaptation, which is often infeasible due to privacy regulations. Multi-source-free unsupervised domain adaptation (MSFDA) addresses this limitation by leveraging multiple labeled source domains to generalize to an unlabeled target domain without requiring access to source data. However, existing MSFDA methods rely on full backbone fine-tuning, leading to catastrophic forgetting and overfitting on small clinical datasets, and address domain shift at the aggregation stage without establishing a shared domain-invariant feature space. Experiments on five publicly available skin lesion datasets show that CAT-CKD achieves an average AUROC of 86.1%, outperforming existing MSFDA methods while requiring only 4.3M trainable parameters. Title: BISECT: Bilevel Spectral Decomposition for Low-Rank Continual Learning

  • Authors: Tom Saliencro, Wen-Hsuan Liu, Rohan Desai, Amara Okonkwo, Maya Lindqvist
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03574
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03574

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Exemplar-free continual learning on pre-trained Vision Transformers increasingly relies on Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), yet prevailing methods govern the low-rank update space through strict subspace isolation. By projecting each incoming task into the approximate null space of historical token activations, isolation-based learners equate any cross-task feature overlap with catastrophic interference. We show that this orthogonality premise induces a structural bottleneck we term \emph{spectral starvation}: when down-projection bases are locked before task optimization, the gradient norm reaching the trainable up-projection is upper-bounded by the energy of current-task features captured by the chosen basis. Because natural visual tasks share low- and mid-level covariance structure, historical null tails contain barely $5.5\%$ of incoming task variance, starving new-class plasticity while blocking positive consolidation along shared directions. We propose BISECT (Bilevel Spectral Decomposition), a continual LoRA framework that partitions a fixed rank-$r$ budget into two orthogonal functional blocks: an invariant carrier spanned by the dominant modes of the joint historical-and-current Gram matrix, and a novelty complement extracted via generalized Rayleigh whitening followed by orthogonal complement projection. After single-task training, BISECT folds both blocks back into the frozen backbone using a closed-form precision gate that scales each rank-1 invariant atom by its current-to-joint Rayleigh ratio while preserving novelty atoms at unit gain. Across eight incremental settings on ImageNet-R, ImageNet-A, CIFAR-100, CUB-200, and DomainNet, BISECT outperforms prompt, adapter, and LoRA baselines with zero extra inference parameters or FLOPs, and remains complementary to sparse analytic expansion.

Title: VeriHarm-Bench: Measuring Harmful Success in LLM Coding Agents Verifiably

  • Authors: Wei-Lin Chen, Zhepei Wei, Xinyu Zhu, Yu-Min Tseng, Tzu-Han Lin, Yu Meng
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03575
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03575
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.03575]] VeriHarm-Bench: Measuring Harmful Success in LLM Coding Agents Verifiably(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03575)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language model (LLM) coding agents increasingly operate with shell access, allowing them to write scripts, move files, alter permissions, and modify system configurations. However, an agent may achieve a user's goal while causing unintended harm, such as deleting unrelated files, weakening access controls, or corrupting data, in response to benign inputs without explicit jailbreaking. This failure mode is particularly important because apparent task success may reassure users that an agent behaved correctly, causing harmful side effects to go unnoticed. We call this harmful success: the agent successfully completes the intended benign task while simultaneously causing unintended harm. Despite its importance, harmful success is difficult to measure because conventional task-success metrics overlook harmful behaviors, while existing safety evaluations are often designed solely to assess harmful outcomes. We introduce VeriHarm, an automatic framework for constructing programmatically verifiable benchmarks that directly measure harmful success. VeriHarm minimally perturbs coding tasks to elicit unintended harmful behavior while maintaining the benignity of the task and preserving the original task objective. It then synthesizes executable harm tests that, together with the original task-correctness tests, evaluate both task accuracy and harmful outcomes. Frontier agents, including GPT-5.6-Sol and Claude-Opus-5, achieve an average task accuracy of 95% on our curated VeriHarm-Bench, yet still struggle to complete tasks safely: their safe success rate, the fraction of tasks completed successfully without triggering the designated harm, averages only 37%. These results suggest that successful task completion can frequently coexist with unintended harm, and that task-success metrics alone may substantially understate the potential safety risks of coding agents.

Our framework improves activation modulation and image realism compared with a recent state-of-the-art method for generating preferred visual representations of these neural targets.

Title: Should We Combine Our Data? Algorithms and Limits for Privately Assessing Data Sharing

  • Authors: Mahdi Haghifam, Kumar Kshitij Patel, Avrim Blum, Giulia Fanti, Juba Ziani
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.DS, cs.IT
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03599
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03599
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.03599]] Should We Combine Our Data? Algorithms and Limits for Privately Assessing Data Sharing(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03599)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: In this paper, we study when two parties should pool their data to train a machine learning model. Two agents, Alice and Bob, each hold a sensitive labeled dataset and want to train a classifier from a given hypothesis class $\mathcal{H}$. Even when each party's dataset can be perfectly classified on its own, the two parties may follow conflicting labeling standards or have selection biases that affect their features and label sets, such that no single classifier in $\mathcal{H}$ fits their combined data. Before committing to data sharing, Alice and Bob therefore want to test whether a single classifier in $\mathcal{H}$ can jointly fit both datasets---without revealing their individual data points to one another. We formalize this problem from a learning-theoretic viewpoint via $\operatorname{opt}_\mathcal{H}$, the minimum \emph{number of mistakes} made by any hypothesis in $\mathcal{H}$ on their pooled data. Through interactive communication, the agents must distinguish $\operatorname{opt}_\mathcal{H}=0$ (compatible datasets) from $\operatorname{opt}_\mathcal{H}\geq t$ (conflicting datasets) under pure $\varepsilon$-two-party differential privacy. A trusted curator could do this with $t=O(1/\varepsilon)$, and we ask how small $t$ can be without one. For thresholds on the real line over a domain of size $m$, we show that the optimal $t$ is $\tilde{\Theta}(\log\log m/\varepsilon)$, exponentially smaller than the $\Theta(\log m/\varepsilon)$ samples needed to privately learn thresholds, even with a curator. For halfspaces in $\mathbb{R}^d$, the dependence on $m$ jumps to $\log(m)$: we give a protocol with $t=\tilde{O}(d^3\log m/\varepsilon)$ and show that some domains require $t=\Omega(d\log(m/d)/\varepsilon)$.

Title: Reviso: Not All Visual Evidence Deserves Equal Attention in Video Captioning

  • Authors: Tengfei Liu, Yang Shi, Yuran Wang, Yuqi Tang, Yizhuo Jia, Zhuoran Zhang, Qixun Wang, Shujie Li, Xuanyu Zhu, Xinyu Liu, Minghui Guo, Shixuan Liu, Haotian Wang, Yuanxing Zhang, Pengfei Wan
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03602
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03602
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.03602]] Reviso: Not All Visual Evidence Deserves Equal Attention in Video Captioning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03602)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate detailed video captions, yet they may overemphasize minor details while overlooking essential context and key events. We argue that not all visual evidence deserves equal attention, as the importance of visual information depends on the content of each video. Motivated by this observation, we propose Reviso, an agentic video captioning framework that learns what to observe and how to gather relevant visual evidence. From a low-frame-rate global preview, Reviso constructs a hierarchical Context--Focus--Points (CFP) observation plan that identifies the video context, prioritizes key aspects, and defines concrete observation targets and corresponding tools. Guided by this plan, Reviso performs goal-directed active perception by selectively revisiting temporal segments and combining tools for fine-grained inspection. It interprets the acquired evidence, updates its plan as needed, and integrates global and local information to generate the final caption. To learn this observation strategy, we develop a verification-guided pipeline that constructs high-quality training trajectories through factual verification and teacher-guided refinement. We further train Reviso using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), incorporating a key-fact reward for caption quality, a Plan Alignment Reward (PAR) for identifying important observation targets, and an Unproductive Tool-Call Penalty (UTCP) that adjusts observation-cost penalties based on caption quality. Experiments across four video captioning benchmarks show that Reviso outperforms the evaluated open-source baselines, while its generated captions better support downstream video question answering. These findings highlight the effectiveness and potential of priority-guided active observation for video captioning.

Title: Efficient Closure Learning in Dynamical Systems: A Comparison of Data-Assimilated and Embedded Approaches

  • Authors: Rileigh Bandy, Teresa Portone, Mohammad Khalil, Eric Crislip, Kyle Neal
  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.DS, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03612
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03612
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.03612]] Efficient Closure Learning in Dynamical Systems: A Comparison of Data-Assimilated and Embedded Approaches(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03612)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Increased computing power has transformed computational science and improved predictive capabilities for complex systems, but many phenomena still exceed current simulation capabilities or require rapid evaluations for fast design iterations. Closure models address this challenge by approximating unresolved effects in reduced or incomplete dynamical systems. Data-driven closures offer a flexible mechanism for improving model accuracy and applicability, but approaches that embed the closure within a computational model for construction and training phases can be computationally expensive and cause numerical instabilities in the computational model during training. To avoid the challenges posed by embedded approaches, staged approaches to closure-model learning have been developed. The first stage uses data assimilation to infer state and closure trajectories from observations. The second stage trains a machine learning closure model offline using the assimilated data, thereby avoiding repeated simulator calls during training. In this work, we investigate the cost and accuracy tradeoffs between embedded and staged data-driven closure modeling approaches for a synthetic epidemiological SIQR model with a nonlinear state-dependent quarantine closure. The results illustrate a practical accuracy-efficiency trade-off: for this exemplar, embedded training provides the strongest average accuracy, while the staged approach yields comparable qualitative behavior with a moderate increase in error, substantially fewer model evaluations, and improved optimization robustness.

Title: Benchmark Prediction with Sparse and Unpaired Cached Responses

  • Authors: Hayden Helm, Avijit Ghosh, Ben Johnson, Carey Priebe
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ME, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03614
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03614
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.03614]] Benchmark Prediction with Sparse and Unpaired Cached Responses(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03614)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Evaluating a generative model on all queries from all available benchmark tasks is prohibitively expensive. As such, models are often evaluated on different subsets of tasks and/or different subsets of queries from a given task. The resulting collection of responses and the corresponding (model, task) score matrix are sparse and unpaired. In this paper, we introduce the Product Kernel Perspective Space (PKPS), a collection of low-dimensional representations of models based on their embedded responses. PKPS generalizes existing methods for representing black-box generative models by comparing responses to similar -- rather than just identical -- queries and is therefore able to utilize sparse and unpaired information. Theoretically, we show that benchmark prediction using PKPS is query-efficient relative to methods that only use information from dense and paired portions of the evaluation data. Empirically, we show that PKPS-based methods outperform baselines on benchmark prediction across two large benchmark suites.

Title: Tracing the Scientific Discovery Capacity of LLMs to Their Training Data

  • Authors: Arda Uzunoglu, Mahler Revsine, Michael Schatz, Benjamin Van Durme, Mehrdad Farajtabar, Daniel Khashabi, Eric Nalisnick
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03617
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03617
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.03617]] Tracing the Scientific Discovery Capacity of LLMs to Their Training Data(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03617)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) show growing promise for scientific discovery, yet basic questions remain about what enables reliable discovery. How can we trace the scientific discovery capacity of LLMs to their training data? We study this question as one of missing probability mass and develop a theoretical account in which monofacts-the facts observed once during training-set the discovery capacity. In materials science and regulatory genomics, we directly manipulate monofact rates in training sets and find that more monofacts consistently yield more discoveries across two open-weight model families, sampling strategies, and reasoning budgets. Further experiments show the relationship cannot be explained by training-set diversity alone and can transfer across scientific domains when paired with in-domain data. Together, our results identify monofact rate in training data as a key factor governing discoveries in language models.

Title: Not All Reasoning Generalizes: A Case Study on Learning Generalizable Reasoning Strategies in RLVR

  • Authors: Ziqi Pang, Yu-Xiong Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03627
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03627
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.03627]] Not All Reasoning Generalizes: A Case Study on Learning Generalizable Reasoning Strategies in RLVR(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03627)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) can enable generalizable improvement for large language model (LLM) reasoning. This paper studies how to incentivize such generalization via reasoning strategies. We study this question with a controllable equation-solving framework: finding multiple inputs x for equations f(x)=y, where f is a non-bijective string function. This setting makes reasoning strategies observable while supporting a clear definition of easy-to-hard out-of-distribution (OOD) shifts, e.g., more solutions, longer y, etc. We uncover a subtle RLVR failure: it teaches the model to solve in-distribution tasks with high accuracy and structured step-by-step derivation, yet under OOD shifts, the same model abandons derivation and collapses to ineffective brute-force enumeration, causing sharp accuracy drops. This observation reveals the deeper challenges of RLVR generalization as it is neither incapable base model nor reward hacking, since the model succeeds in-distribution with the correct reasoning strategy. The key to mitigating this failure is an indirect intervention that surprisingly helps: co-training with bijective inversion tasks. Although these bijective tasks are actually not uniformly harder and even further from the non-bijective OOD evaluation, they make derivation more certain and substantially restore the correct strategy and accuracy under OOD shifts. This effect further motivates our theoretical analysis: outcome-only RLVR can shape reasoning processes through implicitly crediting the effective reasoning strategies more, even without process supervision. To conclude, our analysis framework and findings systematically reveal the crucial role and incentivization of reasoning strategies in RLVR generalization under OOD shifts. Code is available at this https URL.

Title: Pretraining Data Scale Reverses the Muon-AdamW Fine-Tuning Gap

  • Authors: Catherine Li, Jingchu Gai, Andrej Risteski, Jacob Mitchell Springer, Aditi Raghunathan
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03633
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03633
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.03633]] Pretraining Data Scale Reverses the Muon-AdamW Fine-Tuning Gap(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03633)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Muon has recently emerged as a promising optimizer for pretraining large language models, offering a speedup over AdamW in minimizing pretraining loss. However, it remains unclear whether Muon's advantage persists after fine-tuning. In this paper, we study the downstream behavior of Muon and AdamW and uncover a surprising trend: beyond a sufficiently large pretraining data budget, AdamW outperforms Muon after fine-tuning. Moreover, as model size increases, AdamW overtakes Muon at progressively smaller pretraining token-parameter ratios. To explain this reversal, we view fine-tuning as a perturbation of the outputs and introduce the margin-to-spread ratio, which measures how far the correct logit lies above the wrong ones relative to how widely the wrong logits are spread. In language models, Muon has the larger margin-to-spread ratio at small data budgets and AdamW at large ones, mirroring the fine-tuning trend. In a linear associative memory, we prove that after one update the margin-to-spread ratio of gradient descent gains on that of Muon as the data budget grows, and simulations show that this trend persists after extended training.
